In 1961, the same year that Helmsley, Wien, and Malkin had purchased the Empire State Building, the [Port Authority of New York and New Jersey](/wiki/Port_Authority_of_New_York_and_New_Jersey "Port Authority of New York and New Jersey") formally backed plans for a new [World Trade Center](/wiki/World_Trade_Center_\(1973%E2%80%932001\) "World Trade Center \(1973–2001\)") in [Lower Manhattan](/wiki/Lower_Manhattan "Lower Manhattan").[307] The plan originally included 66-story twin towers with column-free open spaces. The Empire State's owners and real estate speculators were worried that the twin towers' 7.6 million square feet (710,000 m2) of office space would create a glut of rentable space in Manhattan as well as take away the Empire State Building's profits from lessees.[308] A revision in the World Trade Center's plan brought the twin towers to 1,370 feet (420 m) each or 110 stories, taller than the Empire State.[309] Opponents of the new project included prominent real-estate developer [Robert Tishman](/wiki/Robert_Tishman "Robert Tishman"), as well as Wien's Committee for a Reasonable World Trade Center.[309] In response to Wien's opposition, Port Authority executive director [Austin J. Tobin](/wiki/Austin_J._Tobin "Austin J. Tobin") said that Wien was only opposing the project because it would overshadow his Empire State Building as the world's tallest building.[310]

The World Trade Center's twin towers started [construction](/wiki/Construction_of_the_World_Trade_Center "Construction of the World Trade Center") in 1966.[311] The following year, the [Ostankino Tower](/wiki/Ostankino_Tower "Ostankino Tower") in [Moscow](/wiki/Moscow "Moscow") succeeded the Empire State Building as the [tallest freestanding structure in the world](/wiki/List_of_tallest_freestanding_structures "List of tallest freestanding structures").[312] In 1970, the Empire State surrendered its position as the world's tallest building,[313] when the World Trade Center's still-under-construction [North Tower](/wiki/1_World_Trade_Center_\(1970%E2%80%932001\) "1 World Trade Center \(1970–2001\)") surpassed it, on October 19;[305][306] the North Tower was [topped out](/wiki/Topping_out "Topping out") on December 23, 1970.[306][314]

In December 1975, the observation deck was opened on the 110th floor of the Twin Towers, significantly higher than the 86th floor observatory on the Empire State Building.[198] The latter was also losing revenue during this period, particularly as a number of broadcast stations had moved to the World Trade Center in 1971; the Port Authority continued to pay the broadcasting leases for the Empire State until 1984.[149] The Empire State Building was still seen as prestigious, having seen its forty-millionth visitor in March 1971.[315]

### 1980s and 1990s

By 1980, there were nearly two million annual visitors,[274] although a building official had previously estimated between 1.5 million and 1.75 million annual visitors.[114] The building received its own ZIP code in May 1980 in a roll out of 63 new postal codes in Manhattan. At the time, its tenants collectively received 35,000 pieces of mail daily.[24] The Empire State Building celebrated its 50th anniversary on May 1, 1981, with a much-publicized, but poorly received, laser light show,[316] as well as an "Empire State Building Week" that ran through to May 8.[317][95] The [New York City Landmarks Preservation Commission](/wiki/New_York_City_Landmarks_Preservation_Commission "New York City Landmarks Preservation Commission") (LPC) voted to designate the building and its lobby as city landmarks on May 19, 1981.[318][319]

Capital improvements were made to the Empire State Building during the early to mid-1990s at a cost of $55 million.[320] Because all of the building's windows were being replaced at the same time, the LPC mandated a paint-color test for the windows; the test revealed that the Empire State Building's original windows were actually red.[321] The improvements also entailed replacing alarm systems, elevators, windows, and air conditioning; making the observation deck compliant with the [Americans with Disabilities Act of 1990](/wiki/Americans_with_Disabilities_Act_of_1990 "Americans with Disabilities Act of 1990") (ADA); and refurbishing the limestone facade.[322] The observation deck renovation was added after disability rights groups and the [United States Department of Justice](/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Justice "United States Department of Justice") filed a lawsuit against the building in 1992, in what was the first lawsuit filed by an organization under the new law.[323][324] A settlement was reached in 1994, in which Empire State Building Associates agreed to add ADA-compliant elements, such as new elevators, ramps, and automatic doors, during the renovation.[324][325]

Prudential sold the property in 1991 for $42 million to a buyer representing hotelier [Hideki Yokoi](/w/index.php?title=Hideki_Yokoi&action=edit&redlink=1 "Hideki Yokoi \(page does not exist\)") [[ja](https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E6%A8%AA%E4%BA%95%E8%8B%B1%E6%A8%B9 "ja:横井英樹")], who was imprisoned at the time in connection with the deadly [Hotel New Japan Fire](/w/index.php?title=Hotel_New_Japan_Fire&action=edit&redlink=1 "Hotel New Japan Fire \(page does not exist\)") [[ja](https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E3%83%9B%E3%83%86%E3%83%AB%E3%83%8B%E3%83%A5%E3%83%BC%E3%82%B8%E3%83%A3%E3%83%91%E3%83%B3%E7%81%AB%E7%81%BD "ja:ホテルニュージャパン火災")] at the [Hotel New Japan](/w/index.php?title=Hotel_New_Japan&action=edit&redlink=1 "Hotel New Japan \(page does not exist\)") [[ja](https://ja.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E3%83%9B%E3%83%86%E3%83%AB%E3%83%8B%E3%83%A5%E3%83%BC%E3%82%B8%E3%83%A3%E3%83%91%E3%83%B3 "ja:ホテルニュージャパン")] in Tokyo.[326] In 1994, [Donald Trump](/wiki/Donald_Trump "Donald Trump") entered into a joint-venture agreement with Yokoi, with a shared goal of breaking the lease on the land in an effort to gain total ownership of the building so that, if successful, the two could reap the potential profits of merging the ownership of the building with the land beneath it.[327] Having secured a half-ownership of the land, Trump devised plans to take ownership of the building itself so he could renovate it, even though Helmsley and Malkin had already started their refurbishment project.[320] He sued Empire State Building Associates in February 1995, claiming that the latter had caused the building to become a "high-rise slum"[290] and a "second-rate, rodent-infested" office tower.[328] Trump had intended to have Empire State Building Associates evicted for violating the terms of their lease,[328] but was denied.[329] This led to Helmsley's companies countersuing Trump in May.[330] This sparked a series of lawsuits and countersuits that lasted several years,[290] partly arising from Trump's desire to obtain the building's master lease by taking it from Empire State Building Associates.[322] Upon Harry Helmsley's death in 1997, the Malkins sued Helmsley's widow, [Leona Helmsley](/wiki/Leona_Helmsley "Leona Helmsley"), for control of the building.[331]

### 21st century

#### 2000s

Following the destruction of the World Trade Center during the September 11 attacks, the Empire State Building again became the [tallest building in New York City](/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_New_York_City "List of tallest buildings in New York City"), but was only the [second-tallest building in the Americas](/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_the_world "List of tallest buildings in the world") after the [Sears (later Willis) Tower](/wiki/Willis_Tower "Willis Tower") in Chicago.[312][332][333] As a result of the attacks, transmissions from nearly all of the city's commercial television and FM radio stations were again broadcast from the Empire State Building.[152] The attacks also led to an increase in security due to persistent terror threats against prominent sites in New York City.[334]

In 2002, Trump and Yokoi sold their land claim to the Empire State Building Associates, now headed by Malkin, in a $57.5 million transaction.[290][335] This action merged the building's title and lease for the first time in half a century.[335] Despite the lingering threat posed by the 9/11 attacks, the Empire State Building remained popular with 3.5 million visitors to the observatories in 2004, compared to about 2.8 million in 2003.[336]

Even though she maintained her ownership stake in the building until the post-consolidation IPO in October 2013, [Leona Helmsley](/wiki/Leona_Helmsley "Leona Helmsley") handed over day-to-day operations of the building in 2006 to Peter Malkin's company.[290][337] In 2008, the building was temporarily "stolen" by the _[New York Daily News](/wiki/New_York_Daily_News "New York Daily News")_ to show how easy it was to transfer the deed on a property, since city clerks were not required to validate the submitted information, as well as to help demonstrate how fraudulent deeds could be used to obtain large mortgages and then have individuals disappear with the money. The paperwork submitted to the city included the names of [Fay Wray](/wiki/Fay_Wray "Fay Wray"), the famous star of _King Kong_ , and [Willie Sutton](/wiki/Willie_Sutton "Willie Sutton"), a notorious New York bank robber. The newspaper then transferred the deed back over to the legitimate owners, who at that time were Empire State Land Associates.[338]

#### 2010s to present

[](/wiki/File:EmpireStateBuildingCU.jpg)The Empire State Building lit in blue and white annually for commencement at [Columbia University](/wiki/Columbia_University "Columbia University") [](/wiki/File:ESBandDowntown2015.jpg)The current [One World Trade Center](/wiki/One_World_Trade_Center "One World Trade Center") (seen in the distance) surpassed the Empire State Building's height on April 30, 2012.

Starting in 2009, the building's public areas received a $550 million renovation, with improvements to the air conditioning and waterproofing, renovations to the observation deck and main lobby,[96] and relocation of the gift shop to the 80th floor.[339][340] About $120 million was spent on improving the [energy efficiency](/wiki/Efficient_energy_use "Efficient energy use") of the building, with the goal of reducing energy emissions by 38% within five years.[340][100] For example, all of the windows were refurbished onsite into film-coated "superwindows" which block heat but pass light.[100][341][342] [Air conditioning](/wiki/Air_conditioning "Air conditioning") operating costs on hot days were reduced, saving $17 million of the project's capital cost immediately and partially funding some of the other retrofits.[341] The Empire State Building won the [Leadership in Energy and Environmental Design](/wiki/Leadership_in_Energy_and_Environmental_Design "Leadership in Energy and Environmental Design") (LEED) Gold for Existing Buildings rating in September 2011, as well as the [World Federation of Great Towers](/w/index.php?title=World_Federation_of_Great_Towers&action=edit&redlink=1 "World Federation of Great Towers \(page does not exist\)")' Excellence in Environment Award for 2010.[342] For the LEED Gold certification, the building's energy reduction was considered, as was a large purchase of [carbon offsets](/wiki/Carbon_offset "Carbon offset"). Other factors included low-flow bathroom fixtures, green cleaning supplies, and use of recycled paper products.[343]

On April 30, 2012, [One World Trade Center](/wiki/One_World_Trade_Center "One World Trade Center") topped out, surpassing the Empire State Building as the city's tallest skyscraper.[333] By 2014, the building was owned by the Empire State Realty Trust (ESRT), with Anthony Malkin as chairman, CEO, and president.[344] The ESRT was a public company, having begun trading publicly on the New York Stock Exchange the previous year.[345] In August 2016, the Qatar Investment Authority (QIA) was issued new fully diluted shares equivalent to 9.9% of the trust; this investment gave them partial ownership of the entirety of the ESRT's portfolio, and as a result, partial ownership of the Empire State Building.[346] The trust's president John Kessler called it an "endorsement of the company's irreplaceable assets".[347] The investment has been described by the real-estate magazine _[The Real Deal](/wiki/The_Real_Deal_\(magazine\) "The Real Deal \(magazine\)")_ as "an unusual move for a sovereign wealth fund", as these funds typically buy direct stakes in buildings rather than real estate companies.[348] Other foreign entities that have a stake in the ESRT include investors from Norway, Japan, and Australia.[347]

A renovation of the Empire State Building was commenced in the 2010s to further improve energy efficiency, public areas, and amenities.[1] In August 2018, to improve the flow of visitor traffic, the main visitor's entrance was shifted to 20 West 34th Street as part of a major renovation of the observatory lobby.[349] The new lobby includes several technological features, including large LED panels, digital ticket kiosks in nine languages, and a two-story architectural model of the building surrounded by two metal staircases.[1][349] The first phase of the renovation, completed in 2019, features an updated exterior lighting system and digital hosts.[349] The new lobby also features free Wi-Fi provided for those waiting.[1][350] A 10,000-square-foot (930 m2) exhibit with nine galleries opened in July 2019.[351][352] The 102nd floor observatory, the third phase of the redesign, reopened to the public on October 12, 2019.[115][116][118] That portion of the project included outfitting the space with floor-to-ceiling glass windows and a brand-new glass elevator.[353] The final portion of the renovations to be completed was a new observatory on the 80th floor, which opened on December 2, 2019.[117][105] In total, the renovation cost $160 million[118] or $165 million and took four years to finish.[117][105]

A comprehensive restoration of the building's mooring and antenna masts also began in June 2019. Antennas on the mooring mast were removed or relocated to the upper mast, while the aluminum panels were cleaned and coated with silver paint.[354][355][356] To minimize disruption to the observation decks, the restoration work took place at night. The project was completed by late 2020.[356]

## Height records

[](/wiki/File:New_York_Bldg._Height_Comparison.svg)Height comparison of several New York City buildings, with Empire State second from left

The world record held by the Empire State Building for the longest was for the tallest skyscraper (to structural height). The building held this distinction for 42 years, being surpassed by the [North Tower](/wiki/1_World_Trade_Center_\(1970%E2%80%932001\) "1 World Trade Center \(1970–2001\)") of the World Trade Center in October 1970.[312][332][357] The Empire State Building was also the [tallest human-made structure](/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_and_structures_in_the_world "List of tallest buildings and structures in the world") in the world before it was surpassed by the [Griffin Television Tower Oklahoma](/wiki/Griffin_Television_Tower_Oklahoma "Griffin Television Tower Oklahoma") (KWTV Mast) in 1954,[358] and the [tallest freestanding structure in the world](/wiki/List_of_tallest_freestanding_structures "List of tallest freestanding structures") until the completion of the [Ostankino Tower](/wiki/Ostankino_Tower "Ostankino Tower") in 1967.[312] An early-1970s proposal to dismantle the spire and replace it with an additional 11 floors, which would have brought the building's height to 1,494 feet (455 m) and made it once again the world's tallest at the time, was considered but ultimately rejected.[359]

With the destruction of the World Trade Center in the September 11 attacks, the Empire State Building again became the [tallest building in New York City](/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_New_York_City "List of tallest buildings in New York City"), and the [second-tallest building in the Americas](/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_the_world "List of tallest buildings in the world"), surpassed only by the [Willis Tower](/wiki/Willis_Tower "Willis Tower") in Chicago. The Empire State Building remained the tallest building in New York until the new [One World Trade Center](/wiki/One_World_Trade_Center "One World Trade Center") reached a greater height in April 2012.[312][332][333] As of 2022[[update]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Empire_State_Building&action=edit), it is the seventh-tallest building in New York City and the [tenth-tallest in the United States](/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_the_United_States "List of tallest buildings in the United States").[360] The Empire State Building is the [49th-tallest in the world](/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_the_world "List of tallest buildings in the world") as of February 2021[[update]](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Empire_State_Building&action=edit).[361] It is also the [eleventh-tallest freestanding structure in the Americas](/wiki/List_of_tallest_freestanding_structures_in_the_world "List of tallest freestanding structures in the world") behind the tallest U.S. buildings and the [CN Tower](/wiki/CN_Tower "CN Tower").[362]

## Incidents

### 1945 plane crash

Main article: [B-25 Empire State Building crash](/wiki/1945_Empire_State_Building_B-25_crash "1945 Empire State Building B-25 crash")

[](/wiki/File:Bomber_Crashed_into_Empire_State_Building_1945.jpg)Wreckage from the [1945 Empire State Building B-25 crash](/wiki/1945_Empire_State_Building_B-25_crash "1945 Empire State Building B-25 crash")

At 9:40 am on July 28, 1945, a [North American B-25 Mitchell](/wiki/North_American_B-25_Mitchell "North American B-25 Mitchell") bomber, piloted in thick fog by Lieutenant Colonel William Franklin Smith Jr.,[390] crashed into the north side of the Empire State Building between the 79th and 80th floors (then the offices of the [National Catholic Welfare Council](/wiki/National_Catholic_Welfare_Council "National Catholic Welfare Council")).[35][56] One engine completely penetrated the building, landing on the roof of a nearby building where it started a fire that destroyed a penthouse.[385][391][392] The other engine and part of the landing gear plummeted down an elevator shaft, causing a fire that was extinguished in 40 minutes. Fourteen people were killed in the incident.[285][198][392] Elevator operator Betty Lou Oliver fell 75 stories and survived, which still holds the [Guinness World Record](/wiki/Guinness_World_Records "Guinness World Records") for the longest survived elevator fall recorded.[393]

Despite the damage and loss of life, many floors were open two days later.[285][286] The crash helped spur the passage of the long-pending [Federal Tort Claims Act](/wiki/Federal_Tort_Claims_Act "Federal Tort Claims Act") of 1946, as well as the insertion of retroactive provisions into the law, allowing people to sue the government for the incident.[394] Also as a result of the crash, the [Civil Aeronautics Administration](/wiki/Civil_Aeronautics_Administration_\(United_States\) "Civil Aeronautics Administration \(United States\)") enacted strict regulations regarding flying over New York City, setting a minimum flying altitude of 2,500 feet (760 m) above sea level regardless of the weather conditions.[395][285]

A year later, on July 24, 1946, another airplane narrowly missed striking the building. The unidentified twin-engine plane scraped past the observation deck, frightening the tourists there.[396]

### 2000 elevator plunge

On January 24, 2000, an elevator in the building suddenly descended 40 stories after a cable that controlled the cabin's maximum speed was severed.[397] The elevator fell from the 44th floor to the fourth floor, where a narrowed elevator shaft provided a second safety system. Despite the 40-floor fall, both of the passengers in the cabin at the time were only slightly injured. After the fall, building inspectors reviewed all of the building's elevators.[398]

### Suicide attempts

Because of the building's iconic status, it and other Midtown landmarks are common locations for suicide attempts.[399] More than 30 people have [attempted suicide](/wiki/Jumper_\(suicide\) "Jumper \(suicide\)") over the years by jumping from the upper parts of the building, with most attempts being successful.[400][401]

The first suicide from the building occurred on April 7, 1931, before it was even completed, when a carpenter who had been laid off went to the 58th floor and jumped.[402] The first suicide after the building's opening occurred from the 86th floor observatory in February 1935, when Irma P. Eberhardt fell 1,029 feet (314 m) onto a [marquee sign](/wiki/Marquee_\(sign\) "Marquee \(sign\)").[403] On December 16, 1943, William Lloyd Rambo jumped to his death from the 86th floor, landing amidst Christmas shoppers on the street below.[404] In the early morning of September 27, 1946, [shell-shocked](/wiki/Post-traumatic_stress_disorder "Post-traumatic stress disorder") Marine Douglas W. Brashear Jr. jumped from the 76th-floor window of the Grant Advertising Agency; police found his shoes 50 feet (15 m) from his body.[405]

On May 1, 1947, [Evelyn McHale](/wiki/Evelyn_McHale "Evelyn McHale") leaped to her death from the 86th-floor observation deck and landed on a [limousine](/wiki/Limousine "Limousine") parked at the curb. Photography student Robert Wiles took a photo of McHale's oddly intact corpse a few minutes after her death. The police found a suicide note among possessions that she left on the observation deck: "He is much better off without me.... I wouldn't make a good wife for anybody". The photo ran in the May 12, 1947, edition of _[Life](/wiki/Life_\(magazine\) "Life \(magazine\)")_ magazine[406] and is often referred to as "The Most Beautiful Suicide". It was later used by visual artist [Andy Warhol](/wiki/Andy_Warhol "Andy Warhol") in one of his prints entitled _Suicide (Fallen Body)_.[407] A 7-foot (2.1 m) mesh fence was put up around the 86th floor terrace in December 1947 after five people tried to jump during three weeks in October and November of that year.[408][409] By then, sixteen people had died from suicide jumps.[408]

Only one person has jumped from the upper observatory: Frederick Eckert of [Astoria, Queens](/wiki/Astoria,_Queens "Astoria, Queens"), who ran past a guard in the enclosed 102nd-floor gallery on November 3, 1932. He jumped a gate leading to an outdoor catwalk intended for [dirigible](/wiki/Airship "Airship") passengers, dying when he landed on the 86th-floor deck.[410]

Two people have survived falls by not falling more than a floor. On December 2, 1979, Elvita Adams jumped from the 86th floor, only to be blown back onto a ledge on the 85th floor by a gust of wind and left with a [broken hip](/wiki/Broken_hip "Broken hip").[411][412][413] On April 25, 2013, a man fell from the 86th floor observation deck, but he landed alive with minor injuries on an 85th-floor ledge where security guards brought him inside and paramedics transferred him to a hospital for a psychiatric evaluation.[414]

### Shootings

Two fatal shootings have occurred in the direct vicinity of the Empire State Building. Abu Kamal, a 69-year-old Palestinian teacher, [shot seven people](/wiki/1997_Empire_State_Building_shooting "1997 Empire State Building shooting") on the 86th floor observation deck during the afternoon of February 23, 1997. He killed one person and wounded six others before committing suicide.[415] Kamal reportedly committed the shooting in response to events happening in [Palestine](/wiki/Palestinian_territories "Palestinian territories") and Israel.[416]

On the morning of August 24, 2012, 58-year-old Jeffrey T. Johnson [shot and killed](/wiki/2012_Empire_State_Building_shooting "2012 Empire State Building shooting") a former co-worker on the building's Fifth Avenue sidewalk. He had been laid off from his job in 2011. Two police officers confronted the gunman, and he aimed his firearm at them. They responded by firing 16 shots, killing him but also wounding nine bystanders. Most of the injured were hit by bullet fragments, although three took direct hits from bullets.[15][417]

## Impact

[](/wiki/File:The_Empire_State_Building_by_Glenn_Odem_Coleman,_oil_on_canvas.jpg)_The Empire State Building_ , [Glenn Odem Coleman](/wiki/Glenn_Coleman_\(painter\) "Glenn Coleman \(painter\)"),  
c. 1931

As the tallest building in the world and the first one to exceed 100 floors, the Empire State Building immediately became an icon of the city and of the nation.[250][32][315] In 2013, _[Time](/wiki/Time_\(magazine\) "Time \(magazine\)")_ magazine noted that the Empire State Building "seems to completely embody the city it has become synonymous with".[418] The historian John Tauranac called it "'the' twentieth-century New York building", despite the existence of taller and more [modernist](/wiki/Modern_architecture "Modern architecture") buildings.[419]

The [New York City Landmarks Preservation Commission](/wiki/New_York_City_Landmarks_Preservation_Commission "New York City Landmarks Preservation Commission") voted to designate the building and its lobby as city landmarks on May 19, 1981,[318][319] citing the historic nature of the first and second floors, as well as "the fixtures and interior components" of the upper floors.[420] The [New York City Planning Commission](/wiki/New_York_City_Planning_Commission "New York City Planning Commission") endorsed the landmark status.[421] The building became a National Historic Landmark in 1986[10][422][423] in close alignment with the New York City Landmarks report.[422] The Empire State Building was added to the [National Register of Historic Places](/wiki/National_Register_of_Historic_Places "National Register of Historic Places") the following year due to its architectural significance.[424]

### Contemporary reception

Early architectural critics also focused on the Empire State Building's exterior ornamentation.[36] Architectural critic Talbot Hamlin wrote in 1931, "That it is the world's tallest building is purely incidental."[425] [George Shepard Chappell](/wiki/George_Shepard_Chappell "George Shepard Chappell"), writing in _[The New Yorker](/wiki/The_New_Yorker "The New Yorker")_ under the pseudonym "T-Square", wrote the same year that the Empire State Building had a "palpably enormous" appeal to the general public, and that "its difference and distinction [lay] in the extreme sensitiveness of its entire design".[36][426] Edmund Wilson of _[The New Republic](/wiki/The_New_Republic "The New Republic")_ wrote that the building's neutral color palette made it "New York's handsomest skyscraper".[321]

Architectural critics also wrote negatively of the mast, especially in light of its failure to become a real air terminal. Chappell called the mast "a silly gesture", and [Lewis Mumford](/wiki/Lewis_Mumford "Lewis Mumford") called it "a public comfort station for migratory birds".[36] Nevertheless, architecture critic [Douglas Haskell](/wiki/Douglas_Haskell "Douglas Haskell") said the Empire State Building's appeal came from the fact that it was "caught at the exact moment of transition—caught between metal and stone, between the idea of 'monumental mass' and that of airy volume, between handicraft and machine design, and in the swing from what was essentially handicraft to what will be essentially industrial methods of fabrication."[427]

### As icon

Early in the building's history, travel companies such as [Short Line Motor Coach Service](/w/index.php?title=Short_Line_Motor_Coach_Service&action=edit&redlink=1 "Short Line Motor Coach Service \(page does not exist\)") and [New York Central Railroad](/wiki/New_York_Central_Railroad "New York Central Railroad") used the building as an icon to symbolize the city.[428] In a 1932 survey of 50 American architects, fourteen ranked the Empire State Building as the United States' best building; the Empire State Building received more votes than any building except the [Lincoln Memorial](/wiki/Lincoln_Memorial "Lincoln Memorial").[429][430] After the construction of the first World Trade Center, architect [Paul Goldberger](/wiki/Paul_Goldberger "Paul Goldberger") noted that the Empire State Building "is famous for being tall, but it is good enough to be famous for being good."[114]

As an icon of the United States, it is also very popular among Americans. In a 2007 survey, the American Institute of Architects found that the Empire State Building was "America's favorite building".[431] The building was originally a symbol of hope in a country devastated by the Depression, as well as a work of accomplishment by newer immigrants.[250] The writer Benjamin Flowers states that the Empire State was "a building intended to celebrate a new America, built by men (both clients and construction workers) who were themselves new Americans."[245] The architectural critic [Jonathan Glancey](/wiki/Jonathan_Glancey "Jonathan Glancey") refers to the building as an "icon of American design".[363] Additionally, in 2007, the Empire State Building was first on the AIA's List of [America's Favorite Architecture](/wiki/America%27s_Favorite_Architecture "America's Favorite Architecture").[432]

The Empire State Building has been hailed as an example of a "[wonder of the world](/wiki/Wonders_of_the_World "Wonders of the World")" due to the massive effort expended during construction.[419] _[The Washington Star](/wiki/The_Washington_Star "The Washington Star")_ listed it as part of one of the "seven wonders of the modern world" in 1931, while _[Holiday](/wiki/Holiday_\(magazine\) "Holiday \(magazine\)")_ magazine wrote in 1958 that the Empire State's height would be taller than the combined heights of the [Eiffel Tower](/wiki/Eiffel_Tower "Eiffel Tower") and the [Great Pyramid of Giza](/wiki/Great_Pyramid_of_Giza "Great Pyramid of Giza").[419] The [American Society of Civil Engineers](/wiki/American_Society_of_Civil_Engineers "American Society of Civil Engineers") also declared the building "A Modern Civil Engineering Wonder of the United States" in 1958[297] and one of the [Seven Wonders of the Modern World](/wiki/Seven_Wonders_of_the_Modern_World "Seven Wonders of the Modern World") in 1994.[433] Ron Miller, in a 2010 book, also described the Empire State Building as one of the "seven wonders of engineering".[434] It has often been called the [Eighth Wonder of the World](/wiki/Eighth_Wonder_of_the_World "Eighth Wonder of the World") as well, an appellation that it has held since shortly after opening.[197][283][435] The panels installed in the lobby in 1963 reflected this, showing the seven original wonders alongside the Empire State Building.[97] The Empire State Building also became the standard of reference to describe the height and length of other structures globally, both natural and human-made.[436]

The building has also inspired replicas. The [New York-New York Hotel and Casino](/wiki/New_York-New_York_Hotel_and_Casino "New York-New York Hotel and Casino") in [Paradise, Nevada](/wiki/Paradise,_Nevada "Paradise, Nevada"), contains the "Empire Tower",[437] a 47-story replica of the Empire State Building.[438][439] A portion of the hotel's interior was also designed to resemble the Empire State Building's interior.[438]

### In media

Main article: [Empire State Building in popular culture](/wiki/Empire_State_Building_in_popular_culture "Empire State Building in popular culture")

As an icon of New York City, the Empire State Building has been featured in various films, books, TV shows, and video games. According to the building's official website, more than 250 movies contain depictions of the Empire State Building.[440] In his book about the building, John Tauranac writes that its first documented appearance in popular culture was _Swiss Family Manhattan_ , a 1932 children's story by [Christopher Morley](/wiki/Christopher_Morley "Christopher Morley").[441] A year later, the film _[King Kong](/wiki/King_Kong_\(1933_film\) "King Kong \(1933 film\)")_ depicted [Kong](/wiki/King_Kong "King Kong"), a giant [stop motion](/wiki/Stop_motion "Stop motion") ape that climbs the Empire State Building during the film's climax,[259][260][442] bringing the building into the popular imagination.[56][442] Later movies such as _[An Affair to Remember](/wiki/An_Affair_to_Remember "An Affair to Remember")_ (1957), _[Sleepless in Seattle](/wiki/Sleepless_in_Seattle "Sleepless in Seattle")_ (1993), and _[Independence Day](/wiki/Independence_Day_\(1996_film\) "Independence Day \(1996 film\)")_ (1996) also prominently featured the building.[443][440] The building has also been featured in other works, such as "[Daleks in Manhattan](/wiki/Daleks_in_Manhattan "Daleks in Manhattan")", a 2007 episode of the TV series _[Doctor Who](/wiki/Doctor_Who "Doctor Who")_ ;[443] and _[Empire](/wiki/Empire_\(1964_film\) "Empire \(1964 film\)")_ , an eight-hour black-and-white [silent film](/wiki/Silent_film "Silent film") by [Andy Warhol](/wiki/Andy_Warhol "Andy Warhol"),[443] which was later added to the [Library of Congress](/wiki/Library_of_Congress "Library of Congress")'s [National Film Registry](/wiki/National_Film_Registry "National Film Registry").[444]

Throughout its history, the Empire State Building has welcomed celebrities, royalty, and dignitaries to visit the observation deck. From celebrities like [Taylor Swift](/wiki/Taylor_Swift "Taylor Swift") and [Zendaya](/wiki/Zendaya "Zendaya") to royalty such as [Prince William](/wiki/William,_Prince_of_Wales "William, Prince of Wales"), the Empire State Building hosts notable figures every year.[445]

### Empire State Building Run-Up

The Empire State Building Run-Up, a foot race from ground level to the 86th-floor observation deck, has been held annually since 1978.[446] It is organized by [NYCRUNS](/wiki/NYCRUNS "NYCRUNS").[447] Its participants are referred to both as runners and as climbers, and are often [tower running](/wiki/Tower_running "Tower running") enthusiasts. The race covers a vertical distance of 1,050 ft (320 m) and takes in 1,576 steps. The record time is 9 minutes and 33 seconds, achieved by Australian professional cyclist [Paul Crake](/wiki/Paul_Crake "Paul Crake") in 2003, at a climbing rate of 6,593 ft (2,010 m) per hour.[448][449]

## Notes

  1. **^** The tenants' entrance is located at 350 Fifth Avenue, while the visitors' entrance is located at 20 West 34th Street.[1]
  2. ^ _**a**_ _**b**_ The Empire State Building is located within the 10001 zip code area,[23] but 10118 has been assigned as the building's own zip code by the [United States Postal Service](/wiki/United_States_Postal_Service "United States Postal Service")[20][21] since 1980.[24]
  3. ^ _**a**_ _**b**_ Most sources state that there are 102 floors,[5][6][7] but some give a figure of 103 floors due to the presence of a balcony above the 102nd floor.[15][16] See § Opening and early years and § Above the 102nd floor for a detailed explanation.
  4. ^ _**a**_ _**b**_ Per the 1916 Zoning Act, the wall of any given tower that faces a street could only rise to a certain height, proportionate to the street's width, at which point the building had to be set back by a given proportion. This system of setbacks would continue until the tower reaches a floor level in which that level's floor area was 25% that of the ground level's area. After that 25% threshold was reached, the building could rise without restriction.[42][43][44] The 1916 Zoning Act was amended in 1961 so that buildings erected thereafter could not exceed a [floor area ratio](/wiki/Floor_area_ratio "Floor area ratio") that was calculated for each zoning district.[45] The maximum ratio for the Empire State Building's district is 15, unless it includes a public plaza.[46] A [grandfather clause](/wiki/Grandfather_clause "Grandfather clause") permits preexisting structures to continue under the old rule. Therefore, the Empire State Building's floor area ratio of 25 cannot be duplicated, or even approached, by a new building in that district.[47]
  5. ^ _**a**_ _**b**_ See Landmarks Preservation Commission Interior 1981, PDF page 26, for a diagram of the lobby.
  6. ^ _**a**_ _**b**_ _**c**_ _**d**_ The 101st floor was later renamed the 102nd floor and is 101 floors above ground. The former 102nd floor, now the 103rd floor, is now a balcony that is off-limits to the public, and is 102 floors above ground.[279]
  7. **^** These proposals included the 100-story [Metropolitan Life North Building](/wiki/Metropolitan_Life_North_Building "Metropolitan Life North Building"); a 1,050-foot (320 m) tower built by [Abraham E. Lefcourt](/wiki/Abraham_E._Lefcourt "Abraham E. Lefcourt") at Broadway and 49th Street; a 100-story tower developed by the [Fred F. French](/wiki/Fred_F._French "Fred F. French") Company on Sixth Avenue between 43rd and 44th streets; an 85-story tower to be developed on the site of the Belmont Hotel near Grand Central Terminal; and the Noyes-Schulte Company's proposed tower on Broadway between Duane and Worth streets. Only one of these projects was even partially completed: the base of the Metropolitan Life North Building.[186]

1931–1967  | Succeeded by[Ostankino Tower](/wiki/Ostankino_Tower "Ostankino Tower")  
**[Tallest building in the world](/wiki/Skyscraper#History_of_the_tallest_skyscrapers "Skyscraper") **  
1931–1970  | Succeeded by[World Trade Center (1973–2001) (North Tower)](/wiki/World_Trade_Center_\(1973%E2%80%932001\) "World Trade Center \(1973–2001\)")  
**[Tallest building in the United States](/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_the_United_States "List of tallest buildings in the United States") **  
1931–1970   
**[Tallest building in New York City](/wiki/List_of_tallest_buildings_in_New_York_City "List of tallest buildings in New York City") **  
1931–1972

2001–2012  | Succeeded by[One World Trade Center](/wiki/One_World_Trade_Center "One World Trade Center") (current)
